TL;DR: This paper computes relativistic dynamical friction and accretion on compact objects in EMRIs around Kerr black holes embedded in dark matter spikes, revealing that frame-dragging in rapidly spinning BHs can reverse the friction sign near the ISCO — a phenomenon not captured by local-density approximations like Chandrasekhar’s formula.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To accurately model dark matter (DM) effects on extreme-mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) observed by LISA, particularly how DM spikes around massive black holes influence orbital evolution via dynamical friction and accretion — going beyond Schwarzschild approximations and local-density prescriptions.

Method: Computes relativistic dynamical friction and accretion for a non-spinning compact object on circular equatorial orbits around a Kerr black hole embedded in a collisionless DM spike; the DM spike is modeled as adiabatically grown from an initial Hernquist profile; momentum transfer is obtained by integrating relativistic scattering and capture cross sections over the full, anisotropic phase space (not just local density); compares results against Chandrasekhar-like approximations.

Result: Frame dragging in rapidly spinning BHs (a ≳ 0.37) forces bound DM particles to co-rotate, causing the secondary to be overtaken by the DM medium near the ISCO — leading to weakening and sign reversal of dynamical friction where DM density peaks; similar reversal can occur for non-rotating BHs only with unphysically large initial halo angular momentum/anisotropy; Chandrasekhar-like prescriptions overestimate friction because they ignore phase-space structure.

Conclusion: Modeling DM effects on EMRIs requires full phase-space treatment of the DM environment; local-density approximations are insufficient, especially near rapidly spinning BHs where relativistic orbital and frame-dragging effects qualitatively alter dynamical friction.

Abstract: Extreme-mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) observed by LISA will probe the environments of massive black holes (BHs), including the dark matter (DM) overdensities expected to form around them. We compute the relativistic dynamical friction and accretion experienced by a non-spinning compact object on circular equatorial orbits around a Kerr BH embedded in a collisionless DM distribution, extending previous results in Schwarzschild. The DM is modeled as a spike grown adiabatically from an initial Hernquist profile, and the momentum transfer is obtained by integrating relativistic scattering and capture cross sections over the full, generically anisotropic phase space seen by the secondary. Far from the primary the friction tracks the local DM density, which increases with spin. Near the innermost stable circular orbit of rapidly spinning BHs $(a\gtrsim 0.37)$, however, frame dragging forces bound DM particles to co-rotate, so that the secondary is overtaken by the surrounding medium: the friction weakens and eventually reverses sign where the DM density peaks. We show that a similar reversal can be mimicked around a non-rotating BH if the initial halo carries large net angular momentum, although this requires anisotropies beyond those found in cosmological simulations. We also show that commonly used Chandrasekhar-like prescriptions, which depend only on the local density, can overestimate the friction force. Our results demonstrate that the full phase-space structure of the environment must be included when modeling DM effects on EMRIs.

</details>


### [168] [Enhancing eccentricity through tidal resonance in binary neutron star systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31172)
*Ivan Markin,Natalie Williams,Tim Dietrich*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that orbital eccentricity in binary neutron star systems can arise during late inspiral due to f-mode tidal resonance with a retrograde-spinning star—not solely from dynamical formation history—via angular momentum transfer that alters the star's spin, explainable by resonance locking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reconcile measurable orbital eccentricity in binary neutron stars with formation scenarios, particularly since isolated binaries are expected to circularize before merger; the authors investigate whether late-time astrophysical mechanisms (e.g., tidal resonances) could generate eccentricity independently of formation environment.

Method: High-resolution numerical-relativity simulations of merging binary neutron stars with high spins—one prograde, one retrograde—combined with analysis of f-mode resonance dynamics and angular momentum transfer; validated against a resonance locking model incorporating spin-dependent f-mode frequency shifts.

Result: Persistent f-mode resonance with the retrograde-spinning star is observed during late inspiral, driving gradual spin-down of that star via orbital angular momentum transfer; this induces measurable orbital eccentricity; the spin evolution matches predictions of the resonance locking model.

Conclusion: f-mode tidal resonance in rapidly spinning, asymmetric binaries provides a novel, formation-history-independent mechanism for generating orbital eccentricity in binary neutron stars—impacting gravitational-wave modeling and interpretation of eccentricity measurements.

Abstract: Orbital eccentricity of binary neutron star systems is a key parameter to reveal their formation history. Since binaries formed in isolation are expected to circularize long before merger, measurable eccentricity suggests dynamical formation in dense stellar environments. However, using numerical-relativity simulations, we show that eccentricity can be introduced in the late inspiral of an initially quasicircular binary, due to the $f$-mode resonance of one of the stars. We simulate the merger of two highly spinning neutron stars, one star spinning prograde and the other retrograde. Persistent tidal resonance with the $f$ mode of the retrograde-spinning star is observed, accompanied by a gradual change of its spin due to angular momentum transfer from the orbit. This spin change can be well explained by a simple resonance locking model that accounts for the spin-dependent shift of the $f$-mode frequency. These findings reveal an origin of eccentricity in binary neutron stars that is independent of their formation history and provide important information for modeling binary neutron star dynamics with rapidly spinning stars.

</details>


### [169] [Optimal Statistic for a Stochastic Gravitational Wave Background using Pulsar-Timing--Astrometry Cross Correlations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31196)
*Kimberly K. Boddy,Jeffrey S. Hazboun,Kris Pardo*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper develops correlation statistics for detecting stochastic gravitational wave backgrounds using astrometric measurements of stellar angular deflections, draws formal analogy to pulsar timing, and introduces cross-correlation between astrometry and pulsar timing data; it estimates signal-to-noise ratios and provides scaling laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend gravitational wave detection beyond pulsar timing by leveraging astrometric measurements, which offer overlapping and higher-frequency GW coverage via transverse stellar deflections.

Method: Develops an auto-correlation statistic for stellar angular deflections (analogous to pulsar timing residual correlations) and a cross-correlation statistic between stellar deflections and pulsar timing residuals, all within a unified formalism; derives signal-to-noise ratios and scaling laws for weak- and intermediate-signal regimes.

Result: Formal framework established for GW detection via astrometry; auto- and cross-correlation statistics derived; analytical signal-to-noise estimates and scaling laws obtained for stochastic GW background searches.

Conclusion: Astrometric measurements are a viable and complementary probe for stochastic gravitational wave backgrounds; the unified formalism enables joint analysis with pulsar timing, enhancing sensitivity and frequency coverage.

Abstract: Astrometric measurements have emerged as a viable probe of gravitational waves (GWs), complementing pulsar timing searches by providing overlapping and higher GW frequency coverage. At its core, the search for GWs using astrometry is very similar to the search with pulsar timing data: GWs induce a change in pulse arrival times along the line of sight to a pulsar and a transverse angular deflection in the apparent position of a star. In analogy to pulsar timing, we develop a correlation statistic between pairs of stellar deflections for astrometry to search for a stochastic GW background. With a common formalism for both astrometry and pulsar timing, we also develop the cross-correlation statistic between stellar deflections and pulsar timing residuals. We estimate the signal-to-noise ratios from the correlation statistics and provide scaling laws in the weak- and intermediate-signal regimes.

</details>


### [170] [On the existence of constants of motion for multipolar test-body dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31200)
*Paul Ramond*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper derives a Hamiltonian formulation for the conservative dynamics of an extended test body with arbitrary-spin and tidal multipoles in curved spacetime, proves exact conservation of mass and spin norm, constructs high-order Carter–Rüdiger constants for Kerr black holes up to hexadecapolar (quartic-in-spin) order—showing integrability only when the body's multipoles match those of a Kerr black hole—and demonstrates breakdown of such constants at quintic-in-spin order, revealing a dynamical hidden symmetry unique to black-hole binaries that persists to high but finite multipolar order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether spinning compact objects in Kerr spacetimes admit hidden symmetries beyond geodesic motion, and to determine whether integrability extends beyond quadrupole order—especially whether it distinguishes black holes from other compact objects via their multipole structure.

Method: Covariant Hamiltonian formalism for extended test bodies with arbitrary-order spin-induced and adiabatic tidal multipoles; derivation of conserved quantities from symmetry of the Hamiltonian flow; explicit construction of higher-order constants of motion in Kerr spacetime using Killing tensors and multipole matching; analytic and numerical verification of existence/nonexistence of constants at quintic-in-spin order.

Result: A universal Hamiltonian H = −½μ² + μU governs conservative dynamics, with U encoding all multipoles covariantly; exact conservation of kinematic mass μ and spin norm; existence of Carter–Rüdiger-type constants up to hexadecapolar (quartic-in-spin) order iff the body’s multipoles match those of a Kerr black hole; no such constants exist at quintic-in-spin order for any conservative multipole structure.

Conclusion: Black-hole binaries possess a perturbative dynamical hidden symmetry—manifested as high-order constants of motion—that is unique among compact objects and persists up to quartic-in-spin order, but breaks at quintic order, indicating a fundamental limitation to integrability in the conservative test-body limit.

Abstract: We show that the conservative equations of motion of an extended test body, with force and torque sourced by spin-induced and adiabatic tidal multipoles of arbitrary order, are the flow of the Hamiltonian $H=-\tfrac12\mu^2+\mu U$, where $\mu$ is the body's kinematic mass and all multipoles enter through a covariant scalar potential $U$. For such bodies, a certain notion of mass and a spin norm are shown to be exactly conserved, at all multipolar orders and in any background. We then apply our framework to the Kerr spacetime, and construct extensions of the Carter and Rüdiger constants through hexadecapolar order (quartic in spin). They are found to exist if, and only if, the body's multipoles are those of a Kerr black hole. Together with the energy and angular momentum, they make the dynamics of a spinning test black hole in Kerr perturbatively integrable through that order. Lastly, at the next, quintic-in-spin order, an analytic criterion implemented numerically reveals that neither constant admits a continuation, for any conservative multipoles. In the conservative, test-body limit, black-hole binaries thus carry a dynamical hidden symmetry shared by no other compact objects, which persists to high multipolar order but ultimately breaks.

</details>
